Although the right panel seems brighter, both plots contain an equal amount of or information about the topology of the Universe, as confirmed by both resulting in the same KL divergence.

We solve these equations by developing a special-purpose Python code.
To obtain the transfer function
\Delta_{\ell}(k)
and the primordial power spectrum
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{R}}(k)
, we use
CAMB
with the
Planck
2018 best-fit
\Lambda\textrm{CDM}
cosmological parameters
[
82
]
.
Before we present the covariance matrices for all the compact, orientable topologies, we note that the choice of coordinate system will affect the appearance and symmetries of the covariance matrix.
We will, in this paper, plot the
rescaled
covariance matrix, which we define as

\Xi^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\equiv\frac{C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{C^{\Lambda\mathrm{CDM}}_{\ell}C^{\Lambda\mathrm{CDM}}_{\ell^{\prime}}}},

(5.5)

and will be of importance later when we discuss the KL divergence.

In
Fig.

3
, we show absolute values of the rescaled covariance matrix for the unshifted and untilted
E_{2}
in the coordinate system used for this work.
In the presented plots, we order the
\{\ell,m\}
elements as
s=\ell(\ell+1)+m
, with
m
in the range
-\ell\leq m\leq\ell
.
In the right panel of the figure, we randomly rotate the coordinate system by applying the Wigner D-matrices
D^{\ell}_{m\bar{m}}(\theta_{0},\phi_{0})
(see, e.g., Ref.
[
83
]
).
Here, the new coordinates are
\theta^{\prime}=\theta+\theta_{0}
and
\phi^{\prime}=\phi+\phi_{0}
so that the covariance matrix in the new coordinates becomes

C^{\prime E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}=\sum^{\ell}_{\bar{m}=-\ell}\sum^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\bar{m}^{\prime}=-\ell^{\prime}}D^{\ell}_{m\bar{m}}(\theta_{0},\phi_{0})D^{*\ell^{\prime}}_{m^{\prime}\bar{m}^{\prime}}(\theta_{0},\phi_{0})C^{E_{i}}_{\ell\bar{m}\ell^{\prime}\bar{m}^{\prime}}.

(5.6)

The rotation operation only mixes the
m
and
m^{\prime}
elements in each
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
block, meaning that all the dark, uncorrelated elements in the
\ell-\ell^{\prime}\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\ 2)
of the left panel of
Fig.

3
stay uncorrelated after the rotation.
Unlike this configuration for
E_{2}
, some topologies will induce correlations in all
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
blocks, meaning that a random rotation of the coordinate system can induce correlations between all combinations of
\{\ell,m\}
and
\{\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime}\}
.

Figure 4
:
Left panel:
Absolute values of the rescaled CMB temperature covariance matrix
\Xi^{E_{i};\,\mathrm{TT}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
(at low multipoles
\ell
) for the cubic, untilted
E_{1}
–
E_{6}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{circle}}
and for an on-axis observer.

Right panel:
The two KL divergences
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p||q)
and
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q||p)
, and the off-diagonal signal-to-noise ratio
\sigma_{\tilde{R}}^{-2}
for the cubic, untilted
E_{1}
–
E_{5}
with an on-axis observer.
We find that the curves for
E_{1}
–
E_{3}
are indistinguishable, meaning that the
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
cases carry the same information. The same is true for
E_{4}
and
E_{5}
.
We have varied all lengths
L
and have set
\ell_{\textrm{max}}=30
.

The absolute values of the rescaled covariance matrices for the untilted and unshifted (or on-axis) topologies
E_{1}
–
E_{6}
with topological length scales equal to
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
are shown in the left panel of
Fig.

4
.
All these topologies have non-zero correlations for the
\ell-\ell^{\prime}\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\ 2)
blocks in their on-axis and untilted configurations, but we also find correlations in the
\ell-\ell^{\prime}=
odd blocks of
E_{3}
,
E_{4}
, and
E_{5}
. This can be explained by the lack of symmetry in the
z
-direction.
For
E_{2}
, the
z\rightarrow z\pm L_{B}
transformations both give a
180^{\circ}
corkscrew rotation, preserving the
z
-symmetry when the observer is on-axis.
But
E_{3}
,
E_{4}
, and
E_{5}
do not have this symmetry as the transformation
z\rightarrow z+L_{B}
gives the positive corkscrew rotations of
90^{\circ}
,
120^{\circ}
, and
60^{\circ}
, respectively, while the transformation in the opposite direction
z\rightarrow z-L_{B}
gives a negative rotation, breaking the
z
-direction symmetry.
Both
E_{2}
and
E_{6}
lose this symmetry when they are off-axis, creating
\ell-\ell^{\prime}=
odd correlations.

E_{1}
, on the other hand, will never have
\ell-\ell^{\prime}=
odd correlations.

We can learn something about the symmetries between
(m,m^{\prime})
elements in the covariance plots. It is straightforward to see that an on-axis observer of untilted
E_{2}
,
E_{3}
,
E_{4}
, and
E_{5}
has

\xi^{E_{i};\bm{\hat{k}}_{\bm{n}}}_{k_{\bm{n}}\ell m}\propto\sqrt{\lambda^{E_{i}}}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{mn_{3}\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})},

(5.7)

where
\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})
indicates that the Kronecker delta is 1 if
m=n_{3}\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})
.
Here the integer
\lambda^{E_{i}}
is the number of times one has to translate by
z\rightarrow z+L_{B}
to get a corkscrew rotation of
360^{\circ}
,
namely,
\lambda^{E_{2}}=2
,
\lambda^{E_{3}}=4
,
\lambda^{E_{4}}=3
, and
\lambda^{E_{6}}=6
.
By combining the two eigenmodes that enter the sum in the covariance matrix, we find

\displaystyle\xi^{E_{i};\bm{\hat{k}}_{\bm{n}}}_{k_{\bm{n}}\ell m}\xi^{E_{i};\bm{\hat{k}}_{\bm{n}}*}_{k_{\bm{n}}\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\propto\lambda^{E_{i}}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{mn_{3}\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}n_{3}\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})}=\lambda^{E_{i}}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{mm^{\prime}\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{n_{3}m\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})}.

(5.8)

This then results in a clear pattern in the covariance matrix,

C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\propto\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{mm^{\prime}\,(\mathrm{mod}\ \lambda^{E_{i}})}
.
As mentioned, this pattern will likely disappear once we rotate the coordinate system.

There are other patterns, such as an untilted, cubic
E_{1}
being invariant under a
\pi/4
rotation, yielding only
m-m^{\prime}\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\ 4)
correlations, but we will not find and categorize all these symmetries in this work.

5.2
KL divergence

Searching for repeating circles in the temperature fluctuations of the CMB has been a widely used method to look for evidence for a non-trivial cosmic topology.
However, when the Dirichlet domain does not fully contain the last scattering surface, no circles appear in the CMB and other methods must be applied to find evidence for non-trivial topologies.
We can then ask the question “at what size of the Dirichlet domain will a non-trivial topology of the Universe be indistinguishable from the trivial topology, i.e., from the covering space?”

To answer this question we can apply the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence method
[
84
,
85
]
to a given set of “data”
\{a_{\ell m}\}
to compare the probability distribution of a non-trivial topology,
p(\{a_{\ell m}\})
, to that of the trivial one,
q(\{a_{\ell m}\})
.
We imagine an ideal experiment with no noise, no foreground emission, and no mask to assess the maximum detectability of topology from the CMB.
The KL divergence
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p||q)
, which quantifies the loss of information if we assume the (“correct”) model
p
is represented by the (“incorrect”) model
q
, is given as

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p||q)=\int\mathrm{d}\{a_{\ell m}\}\,\,p(\{a_{\ell m}\})\ln\left[\frac{p(\{a_{\ell m}\})}{q(\{a_{\ell m}\})}\right]\;.

(5.9)

This can be written in terms of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
of the rescaled covariance matrix,
\Xi^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}

[
86
]
. The KL divergence then becomes

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p||q)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\left(\ln|\lambda_{i}|+\lambda_{i}^{-1}-1\right).

(5.10)

We could also turn the question around: “how much information is lost if we assume the model
q
is represented by the model
p
?”
As the eigenvalues of the inverse of the matrix
\Xi^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
are simply
1/\lambda_{i}
, we find

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q||p)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\left(-\ln|\lambda_{i}|+\lambda_{i}-1\right).

(5.11)

5.3
Cosmic variance signal-to-noise ratio

We can also evaluate the signal-to-noise ratio for detecting the off-diagonal elements of a specific correlation matrix in the ideal case of a full-sky CMB map with a given angular resolution and zero instrumental noise.
In this case, the noise is due to the cosmic variance
[
87
]
from a particular random sky realization, and thus is in principle the best we can do to detect topology from a CMB temperature map.

The spherical-harmonic coefficients are random complex numbers with zero mean,
random phase, and amplitude variance given by

\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\equiv\langle|a_{\ell m}|^{2}\rangle=C_{\ell m\ell m},

(5.12)

ignoring instrumental noise and beam effects as appropriate for a cosmic variance-limited observation. The
a_{\ell m}
satisfy the reality condition
a_{\ell-m}=(-1)^{m}a^{*}_{\ell m}
. For
m\neq 0
, they are described by
two independent scalar Gaussian random variables for their real and imaginary parts, each with variance
\sigma_{\ell m}^{2}/2
, and for
m=0
by a single scalar Gaussian random variable of variance
\sigma_{\ell m}^{2}
.

Products of two different spherical-harmonic coefficients are also complex random variables, which can be written as

a_{\ell m}a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}=C_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\pm S_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\,,

(5.13)

where the
C_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
is the expectation value of
a_{\ell m}a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
, and the standard error term
S_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
is a random complex number with zero mean for both real and imaginary components.
To obtain the standard error, the product
a_{\ell m}a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
can be expanded into its real and imaginary parts; each contains two terms which are products of two random normal variables (the real and/or imaginary parts of
a_{\ell m}
).
We approximate the two as being independent; this is exact for a Gaussian random curvature field in the covering space because of statistical isotropy.
It is a good approximation whenever the correlations between spherical-harmonic coefficients are small compared to their amplitudes, which is the case for all the
E_{i}
topologies, except, possibly, for low
\ell
values.
It also appears to be the case for the observed sky when analyzed assuming statistical isotropy, again except, possibly, for low
\ell
values.
Since the variance of the product of two independent random normal variables is the product of the variances, we have

\displaystyle\Var\left(\Re\,a_{\ell m}\Re\,a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\left(1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\right)\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}^{2}\left(1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}\right),

\displaystyle\Var\left(\Re\,a_{\ell m}\Im\,a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\left(1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\right)\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}^{2}\left(1-\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}\right),

(5.14)

and the same result for the other possible combinations of real and imaginary parts on the left side.
Combining these,

\displaystyle\Var\Re\left(a_{\ell m}a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\sigma^{2}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\left(1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}\right),

\displaystyle\Var\Im\left(a_{\ell m}a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\sigma^{2}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\left(1-\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}\right).

(5.15)

Note that the random variable
a_{\ell m}a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
is not itself a Gaussian variable (its amplitude is distributed as the Bessel function
K_{0}
), but this is not important for our conclusions.

To estimate the possible signal-to-noise ratio with which a given topology can be detected, we write the off-diagonal signal of a particular topology as
RC^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
with
R=1
corresponding to the actual signal. We can then estimate
R
from the off-diagonal elements, where each individual element can serve as a noisy estimator
\tilde{R}
of
R
; for example,

\tilde{R}^{\Re}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}=\Re\left(a_{\ell m}a^{*}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)/\Re C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}

or the same with the imaginary component (except for
m=m^{\prime}=0
where the latter vanishes).
We can also estimate
R
using any weighted normalized sum of the individual estimates; a textbook result is that the minimum
variance estimator uses the inverse variance weight for each term. In this case, the inverse variance of the estimate is the sum of the inverse variances of the terms, so

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Var{\tilde{R}}}\equiv\sigma_{\tilde{R}}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\ell m)\neq(\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime})}\frac{1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}-\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left[\frac{2(\Re C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}})^{2}}{\left(1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}\right)\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\sigma^{2}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}}+\frac{2(\Im C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\sigma^{2}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\ell m)\neq(\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime})}\frac{1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}-\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}}{1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}}~\frac{\left|C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\sigma^{2}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\ell m)\neq(\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime})}\frac{\left|C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\ell m}\sigma^{2}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}}.

(5.16)

The sums in
Section

5.3
are over all pairs of indices except along the diagonal where
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
and
m=m^{\prime}
, between
\ell=2
and some
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}
.
The Hermitian redundancy of the correlation matrix means that the sum over-counts the off-diagonal terms by exactly a factor of 2.
This is accounted for by taking
1/2
of the sum, canceling the factors of 2 in each term.
The imaginary-part term vanishes when
m=m^{\prime}=0
. The terms then combine to give the squared amplitude.
We account for the symmetry
{C^{E_{i}}_{\ell-m\ell^{\prime}-m^{\prime}}=(-1)^{m+m^{\prime}}C^{E_{i}}_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}\ell m}}
when neither
m=0
nor
m^{\prime}=0
, with the factor
{(1+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}+\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0}-\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m0}\delta^{(\textrm{K})}_{m^{\prime}0})/2}
in the sum.
Finally, in practice, we ignore the Kronecker delta terms in the final expression, which account for only a fraction
1/\ell^{2}_{\mathrm{max}}
of terms.

The maximum possible signal-to-noise ratio of the off-diagonal elements in the covariance matrix for measuring a given topology is simply
\sigma_{\tilde{R}}^{-2}
, and it
is proportional to the mean-square correlation coefficient of the correlation matrix
C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
.
Section

5.3
can be directly compared with the KL statistics.
This maximum signal-to-noise ratio is obtained if we know the topology and know our position and orientation in the Dirichlet domain.
In practice, we cannot know these things a priori, so we must do a search over a space of locations and orientations for
any particular topology, with only the correct choice giving the maximum signal-to-noise ratio computed here.

5.4
KL divergence and signal-to-noise ratio for compact, orientable, Euclidean manifolds

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p||q)
,

statistics for cubic
E_{1}
as a function of
L_{1}=L_{2}=L_{3}\equiv L
in units of
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
.

In

we compare the three statistics
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p||q)
,
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q||p)
, and
\sigma_{\tilde{R}}^{-2}
for cubic
E_{1}
as a function of
L_{1}=L_{2}=L_{3}\equiv L
in units of
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
, and find that they convey similar information.
In the right panel of
Fig.

4
, we compare the three statistics for the untilted
E_{1}
–
E_{6}
with an on-axis observer.
We find that they qualitatively tell the same story for all cases; once the size of the Dirichlet domain is larger than the diameter of LSS, the information content of the topology of the Universe in the CMB data drops by increasing the length scale and will eventually cross the no-detection line
D_{\mathrm{KL}}=1
.
As these three statistics qualitatively behave similarly, from now on we will only quote
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p||q)
.
However,
the signal-to-noise statistic has the advantage over the KL divergence in that calculating it does not involve inverting a large matrix.
The sums in
Section

5.3
can become unwieldy, but they can be approximated by using a randomly chosen smaller number of terms and scaling, in the style of Monte Carlo integration.

We show in
Figs.

,
7
, and
8
the absolute values of the rescaled covariance matrices
|\Xi^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}|
of each topology for two different configurations: an on-axis observer
\bm{x}_{0}={(0,0,0)}^{T}
and an off-axis observer
\bm{x}_{0}\neq{(0,0,0)}^{T}
.
As the tilt and off-axis position of the observer are degenerate, we set the tilt parameters to zero.
The exception is for
E_{1}
as this topology is homogeneous.
For
E_{1}
we have chosen an untilted (
\beta=90^{\circ}
) configuration and a tilted (
\beta=75^{\circ}
) configuration in
Fig.

6
.

:
Absolute values of the rescaled CMB temperature covariance matrix
\Xi^{E_{i};\,\mathrm{TT}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
(at low multipoles
\ell

E_{1}
and
E_{2}
where we have set
L_{1}=1.4L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
in
E_{1}
and
L_{A}=1.4L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
in
E_{2}
.
The KL divergence is calculated with
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}=30
.
The circle limit for both tilt angles
\beta=90^{\circ}
and
\beta=75^{\circ}
is
L_{2}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
in
E_{1}
and, similarly,

L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
in
E_{2}
. For
E_{2}

L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
, while the observer at
\bm{x}_{0}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}{(0.35,0,0)}^{T}
reaches the circle limit when
L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=\sqrt{1-0.7^{2}}L_{\mathrm{LSS}}\approx 0.71L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
.
For the rescaled covariance matrix plots we have used
L_{2}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}
for
E_{1}
and
L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}
for
E_{2}
.

For
E_{2}
–
E_{5}
, we set
L_{A}=1.4L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
and vary
L_{B}
in the KL divergence plot.
The
x
-axis is shown as
L_{B}/L_{\mathrm{circle}}
, where
L_{\mathrm{circle}}
is defined as the smallest
L_{B}
for which no pairs of identical circles appear in the CMB. For an on-axis observer, this is simply
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
as long as
L_{A}>L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
.
13
13

13

If
L_{A}<L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
there would always be identical circles in the CMB for all values of
L_{B}
.

Hence, when
L_{B}/L_{\mathrm{circle}}>1
no identical circles appear in the CMB and the information content of cosmic topology in the CMB drops as seen by the falling KL divergence.
For the figures of the rescaled covariance matrices, we set
L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}
, meaning that these covariance matrices can never be ruled out by CMB circle searches.
For
E_{1}
, we do the same as above, but with
L_{A}\to L_{1}
and
L_{B}\to L_{2}
.
For
E_{2}
–
E_{6}
,
L_{\mathrm{circle}}
depends on the parameters of the topology, while
E_{1}
always has
L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
as its circle limit even with a tilted topology (
\beta\neq 90^{\circ}
).

The off-axis positions in
Figs.

6
,
7
, and
8
are not randomly chosen.
We have chosen locations that are in the middle between two excluded regions for
L_{B}<L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
of Fig. 2 of Ref.
[
62
]
.
What this means is that the position is chosen so that the observer has the highest number of nearest clones separated by the same distance to the observer.
We, therefore, hypothesize that these off-axis positions represent maximum possible information of non-trivial topology in the CMB.

As in

, but for
E_{3}
and

.

L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}

.

and

and

and

for the observer locations

and

L_{Ax}=L_{By}=L_{Cz}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}
, where
L_{\mathrm{circle}}=1/\sqrt{2}L_{\mathrm{LSS}}\approx 0.71
for
\bm{x}_{0}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}{(0,0,0)}^{T}
and
L_{\mathrm{circle}}\approx 0.90
for
\bm{x}_{0}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}{(-0.5,0.25,0)}^{T}
.
In the KL divergence plot of
E_{5}
, we have set
L_{A}=1.4L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
while varying
L_{B}
. For
E_{6}
, we have varied
L\equiv L_{Ax}=L_{By}=L_{Cz}
, and we have set
r_{x}=r_{y}=r_{z}=1/2
.

For example, for
E_{2}
with
L_{A}=1.4L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
and
\bm{x}_{0}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}{(0.35,0,0)}^{T}
in
Fig.

6
there are four nearest clones with the same distance to the observer.
Namely at positions
\bm{x}={((1.4-0.35)L_{\mathrm{LSS}},0,L_{B})}^{T}
,
\bm{x}={(-0.35L_{\mathrm{LSS}},0,L_{B})}^{T}
,
\bm{x}={((1.4-0.35)L_{\mathrm{LSS}},0,-L_{B})}^{T}
, and
\bm{x}={(-0.35L_{\mathrm{LSS}},0,-L_{B})}^{T}
.
The distance to these four clones is
\sqrt{(0.7L_{\mathrm{LSS}})^{2}+L_{B}^{2}}
.
When the distance is equal to
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
, we get
L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=\sqrt{1-0.7^{2}}L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
.
An on-axis observer
\bm{x}_{0}={(0,0,0)}^{T}
has only two nearest clones,
\bm{x}={(0,0,L_{B})}^{T}
and
\bm{x}={(0,0,-L_{B})}^{T}
, both with a distance
L_{B}
yielding
L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}=L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
.
Fewer nearest clones with the same distance means fewer correlations in the covariance matrix and a lower KL divergence.

E_{6}
is more complex, and we found an off-axis position from numerical considerations.
From
Fig.

8
we already find that the on-axis position contains more information than the chosen off-axis one, meaning that there could be a more optimal off-axis position yielding higher information.

We have also looked into whether including higher multipoles
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}
increases the KL divergence for
E_{1}
to
E_{6}
. Although going to higher multipoles increases the KL divergence when the distance to the nearest clone is less than
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
, we find that increasing
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}=30
to
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}=50
makes a negligible difference when there are no identical circles in the CMB. This agrees with the findings of Ref.
[
28
]
(see the lower right panel of Fig. 2 in that reference).

Figure 9
:
The quantity
C^{E_{i};TT}_{\ell}\equiv\frac{1}{2\ell+1}\sum_{m}C^{E_{i};TT}_{\ell m\ell m}
for the CMB temperature sky for the configurations with off-axis observers in
Figs.

6
,
7
, and
8
. The gray band shows the
1\sigma
cosmic variance for
\Lambda
CDM.

Finally, we show the diagonal
C_{\ell}
in
Fig.

9
, defined as
C_{\ell}\equiv\frac{1}{2\ell+1}\sum_{m}C_{\ell m\ell m}
for the non-trivial topologies. We have divided by
R_{\ell}(|\bm{k}|)=0.99
in
Eq.

5.3
to make up for the power lost by not summing over an infinite number of wavenumbers. These topologies are chosen to have
L_{B}=L_{\mathrm{circle}}
so that their nearest clones are exactly
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
away, meaning that the CMB contains no identical circles.

There are three different ways of generating
a_{\ell m}
realizations of the CMB sky signal assuming a non-trivial topology. As the first possibility, one can sum over all the Fourier modes that contribute to each spherical-harmonic coefficient by generating realizations of the primordial density fluctuations
\delta^{\mathcal{R}}_{\bm{k}_{\bm{n}}}
for each discrete wavenumber
\bm{k}_{\bm{n}}
.
Namely, one would solve

a^{E_{i}}_{\ell m}=\frac{4\pi}{V_{E_{i}}}\sum_{\bm{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{E_{i}}}\delta^{\mathcal{R}}_{\bm{k}_{\bm{n}}}\xi^{E_{i};\bm{\hat{k}}_{\bm{n}}}_{k_{\bm{n}}\ell m}\Delta_{\ell}(k_{\bm{n}}).

(5.17)

As all the information is in the covariance matrix, the second possibility is to generate the
C^{E_{i}}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
and create a realization by using Cholesky decomposition
[
88
,
89
]
.
However, calculating the full covariance matrix is computationally more expensive than performing the summation in
Eq.

5.17
.
A third option is to generate three-dimensional real-space density fluctuations for a given topology and its parameters and then take a spherical slice to create the temperature fluctuations of the CMB.
We expect this to be computationally significantly faster than solving
Eq.

5.17
, especially for topology length scales of the order of or larger than
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
.

5.5
Expectations for three-dimensional versus two-dimensional correlations

We have seen that the reach of the CMB for extracting topological information is likely limited to the case where the distance to our nearest clone is not much more than the diameter of the last scattering surface.
We expect that three-dimensional data from the full interior of the last scattering surface — such as might eventually be obtained using some combination of large-scale structure surveys, intensity mapping, and other data types — would allow us to extend this reach, possibly substantially.

In
Section

4
, we identified two topological effects on eigenmodes that could result in measurable effects on observables — the discretization of the set of allowed wavevectors
\bm{k}_{\bm{n}}
, and, in the non-homogeneous manifolds, the induced correlation between Fourier modes of different allowed wavevectors of the same magnitude.
The observability of the discretization is limited by the observer’s ability to resolve different wavevectors.
The separation between allowed wavevectors is
\sim 2\pi/L_{\mathrm{top}}
, where
L_{\mathrm{top}}
is some characteristic length scale associated with the topology.
The observer’s resolution is limited in principle to
\sim 2\pi/L_{\mathrm{LSS}}
, leading the detectability to fall as
L_{\mathrm{LSS}}/L_{\mathrm{top}}\to\infty
.
However, as the magnitude of the wavevector increases, and the number of modes with the same magnitude of wavevector grows as
k^{2}
, it may become more and more difficult to attain the target angular resolution in the wavevector space, further degrading the ability to detect topological information.

Meanwhile, if we are in an inhomogeneous space in which one (or more) of the group generators involves a rotation about some axis, then all pairs of Fourier modes with wavevectors related by that same rotation have perfectly correlated amplitudes.
This is true no matter the magnitude of the wavevector.
That sounds like a potentially very powerful signal of topology.
However, the phase angle of the correlation grows as the magnitude of the wavevector increases.
For large enough
k
, neighboring wavevectors have essentially random phases.
This will degrade the utility of the correlation information if observations average over nearby wavevectors.

All this needs to be evaluated carefully and is the subject of an upcoming paper
[
90
]
targeted at this and related topics.

6
Conclusion

The local spatial geometry of the Universe may be Euclidean (i.e., flat) when averaged over volumes that are large compared to the largest structures but small compared to our Hubble volume or to the size of the last scattering sphere;
however, that does not mean that the topology is that of the infinite-in-all-directions “covering space” of Euclidean geometry
E^{3}
.
There are eighteen possible topologies (labeled
E_{1}
–
E_{18}
) for three-manifolds that have homogeneous local flat geometry, the covering space is just one of them —
E_{18}
.
In this paper, we have considered the ten topologies that have orientable Euclidean manifolds; in an upcoming paper, we will consider the remaining eight topologies that have non-orientable Euclidean manifolds
[
91
]
.
These ten topologies of orientable manifolds fall into four classes: six with compact manifolds, i.e., all three spatial dimensions are compact, (
E_{1}
–
E_{6}
); two that have two compact dimensions (
E_{11}
and
E_{12}
); one that has one compact dimension (
E_{16}
); and the covering space
E_{18}
, which has no compact dimensions.

For each of these topologies (cf.
Section

3
) we have provided a completely general parametrization of all the possible homogeneous manifolds.
This builds on previous work (see especially Ref.
[
24
]
), which included all these topologies, but not, or not explicitly, with their most general parametrization.
In particular, we have allowed the full range of possibilities for the translation vectors associated with the generators of
E_{2}
–
E_{5}
(that was already the case for
E_{1}
), the two generators of
E_{11}
and
E_{12}
, and the generator of
E_{16}
.
Most new, however, is the recognition that group actions that lead to the so-called slab space
E_{16}
are not generically pure translations, but corkscrew motions; the standard group action based on pure translations is a very special case because it is the only
E_{16}
manifold that is homogeneous.
We have also identified an “associated
E_{1}
” for each manifold of topologies
E_{2}
–
E_{6}
, an associated
E_{11}
for each
E_{12}
manifold, and an associated unrotated, and therefore homogeneous,
E_{16}
(i.e.,
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
) for each rotated
E_{16}
(i.e.,
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}
) where that is possible, i.e, where the rotation is a rational multiple of
2\pi
.

In
Section

4
, we have provided the general analytic expressions for the eigenmodes of the (scalar) Laplacian for each of these orientable manifolds, which are linear combinations of Fourier modes.
Once again, we realize that
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
is a very special case of
E_{16}
.
In considering a generic slab manifold,
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}
, based on a corkscrew motion we find that the rotation associated with that corkscrew must be a rational multiple of
2\pi
in order for the eigenmodes (of the scalar Laplacian) not to have azimuthal symmetry around the corkscrew axis, and thus to be a basis set for general functions on the manifold.
This is a situation unlike any of the other
E^{3}
topologies.

We have also presented the Fourier-mode correlation matrices
C^{E_{i};XY}_{\bm{k}_{\bm{n}}\bm{k}_{\smash{\bm{n}^{\prime}}}}
for the amplitudes of the Fourier modes of the associated
E_{1}
,
E_{11}
, and
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
,
under the assumption that the primordial amplitudes of the eigenmodes of a particular scalar field, e.g., the gauge-invariant curvature potential
\mathcal{R}
, are Gaussian random statistically independent variables of zero mean with a variance that depends only on the eigenvalue of the Laplacian.
The
X
and
Y
label two scalar fields (potentially at some later epoch) that are linearly related to that primordial field through transfer functions
\Delta^{X}(k)
and
\Delta^{Y}(k)
.
(
X
and
Y
are not necessarily different.)
Finally, we have presented, for similar scalar fields “projected” onto the sky, the spherical-harmonic-spherical-harmonic correlation matrix
C^{E_{i};XY}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
for each topology as a function of manifold parameters.
Once again, this builds on previous work, especially Ref.
[
24
]
, but allows for the most general parametrization of each manifold and of the observer’s position therein.

We have explicitly included the effect of the choice of origin
\bm{x}_{0}
on the analytic expressions for the eigenmodes, and the correlation matrices.
This is an important addition because, except for
E_{1}
,
E_{11}
,
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
, and
E_{18}
, the manifolds are inhomogeneous, and the expectations for the distributions of observables depend on the location of an observer in the manifold.

In
Section

5
, we have studied the CMB temperature spherical-harmonic correlation matrices
C^{E_{i};TT}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
for
E_{1}
–
E_{6}
.
We have shown, in
Fig.

3
, how a preferred orientation of the observer concentrates the correlations in a small fraction of the matrix elements, while a random orientation diffuses it broadly across the matrix.
We have suggested for future consideration that this existence of a preferred orientation of the observer may constitute a signature of topology that could be used to identify the presence of a non-trivial topology.
In
Fig.

4
, we have presented the spherical-harmonic correlation matrices for
E_{1}
–
E_{6}
for specific simple choices of the manifold parameters.

Fig.

4
also displays the KL divergence
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(E_{i}||E_{18})
of the usual (diagonal)
E^{3}
covering-space CMB temperature correlation matrix
C^{E_{18};TT}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
given a true underlying correlation matrix
C^{E_{i};TT}_{\ell m\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
of
E_{i}
(
i\neq 18
) (assuming identical cosmological parameters).
This represents the information potentially available from measured CMB temperature fluctuations to distinguish between the possibility that the Universe is an
E_{i}
manifold and the possibility that it is the covering space.
We have also plotted an alternative cosmic variance signal-to-noise statistic (
5.3
) which is more straightforward to calculate for large ranges of
\ell
, and which conveys the same message.
We have considered, but have not displayed,
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(E_{18}||E_{i})
because it contains no clear new information.

These measures of the information content suggest that once the clone-to-last-scattering ratio — the ratio of the distance to an observer’s nearest topological clone to the diameter of the last scattering surface — is greater than 1, the information available to distinguish between an
E_{i}
manifold and the covering space declines rapidly.
In most cases, we find that the “standard” manifold parameter choices and observer locations minimize the available information and that other manifold parameters or observer positions are more favorable for detecting topology.
Nevertheless, in all cases, by the time the clone-to-last-scattering ratio reaches somewhere between 1.1 and 1.3 the KL divergences of the CMB temperature fluctuations have fallen below 1 and there is no prospect of using those fluctuations to differentiate between topologies.
On the one hand, this is disappointing given that we already know observationally (with 95% confidence)
[
25
,
92
]
that this ratio is greater than
0.985
— we might have hoped to explore much larger values of the ratio.
On the other hand, if a non-trivial cosmic topology is the heart of the explanation for large-angle anomalies in the CMB temperature fluctuations
[
93
,
94
,
95
,
15
,
96
,
97
]
, then there
is
significant information about the topology of the Universe in the CMB, teaching us that the clone-to-last-scattering ratio is likely
\lesssim 1.3
, assuming that the insights gained from
E^{3}
apply more broadly.

The CMB is fundamentally poor at determining the amplitudes of individual Fourier modes because the CMB signal is predominantly from the Sachs-Wolfe effect on the last scattering surface, and is thus a measure of the gravitational potential on that spherical shell centered on us.
The projection from the statistically simple correlation between Fourier modes to the correlation between spherical harmonics is certainly not invertible and leads to a considerable loss of information.
We are optimistic that three-dimensional observations (large-scale structure surveys, intensity mapping, …) will not experience the same precipitous drop in KL divergence for clone-to-last-scattering ratios greater than
1
.
This will be explored in an upcoming paper
[
90
]
.

Appendix A
Appendix: Construction of general generators

In this work, the most general allowed set of three generators
g_{a_{j}}
for each of the orientable
E_{i}
has been provided.
Here we describe a constructive, algebraic approach to determining the parametrization of these generators.

The topology of each orientable manifold can be set by the choice of elements of
SO(3)
, i.e., by the matrices
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{i}}_{a}
.
There is limited freedom to adjust the
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{i}}_{a}
: only the freedom to rotate the coordinate system to choose the orientations of the axes of rotation.
14
14

14

The set of matrices is not always unique even up to rotations of the coordinate system.
The Hantzsche-Wendt and non-orientable manifolds can be described by multiple different sets of matrices.
Further, for the non-orientable manifolds the same set of
O(3)
elements
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{i}}_{a}
can be associated with discretely different translation vectors
\bm{T}^{E_{i}}_{a_{j}}
and thus generate topologically distinct manifolds.

The remainder and essence of our task will then be to determine the most general allowed vectors
\bm{T}^{E_{i}}_{a_{j}}
associated with the
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{i}}_{a}
of each
E_{i}
.
Once the orientation of the coordinate system has been set, any remaining freedom will be used to simplify the three vectors
\bm{T}^{E_{i}}_{a_{j}}
.
There are two principal tools available to constrain the components of
\bm{T}^{E_{i}}_{a_{j}}
:

1.

Any finite sequence of generators and their inverses are group elements.
Functionally, this is enforced by checking that any such sequence that is a pure translation is an integer linear combination of some “basis set” of three linearly independent translations, so that the group is a discrete group,
\Gamma^{E_{i}}
.
In
Section

3
we call this basis set (the generators of) “the associated
E_{1}
.”
Part of this process is the determination of the set of such pure translations that can be chosen as the basis set.

For example, consider two generators
g^{E_{n}}_{ai}
and
g^{E_{n}}_{bj}
of
\Gamma^{E_{n}}
for one of the orientable Euclidean manifolds
E_{n}
, associated with the
SO(3)
elements
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{a}
and
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{b}
, respectively, and let
\bm{T}^{E_{n}}_{k}
(
k=1,2,3
) represent the pure translations of the associated
E_{1}
.
Since
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{a})^{-1}(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{b})^{-1}\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{a}\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{b}=\mathds{1}
for all
a
and
b
in all such
\Gamma^{E_{n}}
,
15
15

15

Note that this is not true for arbitrary elements of
O(3)
since it is a non-abelian group. However, it is true for the particular elements of
O(3)
used in the generators for each of the
E_{n}
.

we must insist that

(g^{E_{n}}_{ai})^{-1}(g^{E_{n}}_{bj})^{-1}g^{E_{n}}_{ai}g^{E_{n}}_{bj}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}m_{k}\bm{T}^{E_{n}}_{k}

(A.1)

for some triplet of integers
m_{k}
.

This may not be sufficient.
Consider the case where
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{a}\neq\mathds{1}
but
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{n}}_{a})^{2}=\mathds{1}
and where there are (at least) two generators associated with this matrix.
Then the application of any two of these generators should result in a pure translation.
In other words, all combinations of the form

g^{E_{n}}_{ai}g^{E_{n}}_{aj},\quad g^{E_{n}}_{a{i}}(g^{E_{n}}_{a{j}})^{-1},\quad(g^{E_{n}}_{a{i}})^{-1}g^{E_{n}}_{a{j}},\quad(g^{E_{n}}_{a{i}})^{-1}(g^{E_{n}}_{a{j}})^{-1}

(A.2)

for each of
i,j\in\{1,2\}
must lead to pure translations that are integer linear combinations of the basis set.
If this is true, then (
A.1
) is trivially satisfied.
For some topologies two of these combinations will be used to define the translation vectors and the rest will lead to constraints that must be satisfied.

2.

We must also ensure that the set of transformations (the group elements) consists only of freely acting transformations, i.e., no transformation (other than the identity transformation) has a fixed point.

Once these conditions have been enforced, we may find that there appear to be distinct sets of “solutions,” i.e., parametrizations of the
\bm{T}^{E_{i}}_{a_{j}}
that cannot be transformed into one another by rotations, reorderings, or rescalings.
We must still prove that two such sets do not generate the same lattice of clones for a given starting point.
A simple way that this can happen is if the
\bm{T}^{E_{i}}_{a_{j}}
of one set are just integer linear combinations of the vectors of the other set.

We will show individually that we are able to bring this program to a successful conclusion for each of the orientable manifolds.

A.1
E_{1}
: 3-torus

We have established in the main body of the paper that the three translations for
E_{1}
can be parametrized most generally as

\bm{T}^{E_{1}}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1x}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{1}}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{2x}\\
L_{2y}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{1}}_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{3x}\\
L_{3y}\\
L_{3z}\end{pmatrix},

(A.3)

where the three
0
entries have been fixed through a rotation.
Specifics on the ranges of the parameters are given in the main body.

A.2
E_{2}
: Half-turn space

The half-turn space has one generator,
g_{B}
, that is a corkscrew motion, while the other two,
g_{A_{j}}
(
j=1,2
), are pure translations.
Here we develop the techniques employed in the rest of the orientable manifolds to algebraically determine an action for the most general generators.

To begin we first use two of the rotational degrees of freedom to align the corkscrew axis with the
z
-axis, i.e.,
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}=\diag(-1,-1,0)
, and use the remaining rotational degree of freedom to set
L_{1y}=0
.
Next, we can choose a special origin that allows us to set
L_{Bx}=L_{By}=0
(cf.
Eq.

2.8
).
We will restore an arbitrary origin at the end of the calculation.
With all of this, we can choose our general starting case to be
16
16

16

Here and throughout this appendix we will refrain from adding topology-specific superscripts to quantities until the final results.
All intermediate quantities should be recognized as being specific to the topology under consideration.

\bm{T}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1x}\\
0\\
L_{1z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{2x}\\
L_{2y}\\
L_{2z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.4)

From these, a set of pure translations, an associated
E_{1}
, can be constructed.
The actions of both the
g_{A_{i}}
are already pure translations, so two of the needed translation vectors can be chosen as
\bm{T}_{1}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
and
\bm{T}_{2}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
.
A third translation vector can be defined based on the fact that
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{2}}_{B})^{2}=\mathds{1}
.
Note that

g^{E_{2}}_{3}\equiv(g^{E_{2}}_{B})^{2}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+(\mathds{1}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{2}}_{B})\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{B}.

(A.5)

From this the third translation vector is

\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{3}\equiv(\mathds{1}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{2}}_{B})\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{B}\equiv\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{2}}_{02}\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{B},

(A.6)

for
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{2}}_{02}=\diag(0,0,2)
from (
4.35
).
Thus the third translation vector of the associated
E_{1}
for
E_{2}
is as quoted in the main text (
3.8
),

\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{3}={(0,0,2L_{Bz})}^{T}.

(A.7)

For our subsequent development here we choose
\bm{T}_{3}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{3}
.

The remaining condition to impose (all others are either already pure translations or reduce to this one) is that for
i
and
j\in\{1,2\}

{g}^{-1}_{B}{g}^{-1}_{A_{i}}g_{B}g_{A_{j}}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\bm{T}_{A_{j}}-\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}=\bm{x}+\sum_{k}m^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},

(A.8)

for some set of
m_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
\bm{T}_{A_{i}}
is already one of the pure translations,
\bm{T}_{i}
, this is equivalent to requiring that

\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}=\sum_{k}m^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }m_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z},

(A.9)

leading to the set of conditions

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-L_{1x}\\
\hphantom{-}0\\
\hphantom{-}L_{1z}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}m^{(1)}_{1}L_{1x}+m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2x}\\
m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2y}\\
m^{(1)}_{1}L_{1z}+m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2z}+m^{(1)}_{3}2L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix},

(A.10)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-L_{2x}\\
-L_{2y}\\
\hphantom{-}L_{2z}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}m^{(2)}_{1}L_{1x}+m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2x}\\
m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2y}\\
m^{(2)}_{1}L_{1z}+m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2z}+m^{(2)}_{3}2L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.11)

From the
x
-components of these equations and the fact that
L_{2y}\neq 0
(since at least one of the translation vectors needs to have a nonzero
y
-component) we immediately see that
m_{2}^{(1)}=0
and
m_{2}^{(2)}=-1
, which in turn means that
m_{1}^{(1)}=-1
and
m_{1}^{(2)}=0
, so that we are finally left with

L_{1z}=m^{(1)}_{3}L_{Bz}\mbox{ and }L_{2z}=m^{(2)}_{3}L_{Bz}.

(A.12)

Since
T_{3z}=2L_{Bz}
, we can always subtract integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{3}
, which is equivalent to subtracting
2nL_{Bz}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
from
L_{1z}
and
L_{2z}
, meaning that we only need to consider
m^{(1)}_{3}\in\{0,1\}
and
m^{(2)}_{3}\in\{0,1\}
.
However, if either of
m^{(i)}_{3}
are 1, then
{g}^{-1}_{A_{i}}g_{B}
has a fixed point.
Explicitly, consider the case
m^{(1)}_{3}=1
so that
L_{1z}=L_{Bz}
.
Notice that

{g}^{-1}_{A_{1}}g_{B}:\bm{x}\to\begin{pmatrix}-x-L_{1x}\\
-y\\
\hphantom{-}z\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.13)

and so
{g}^{-1}_{A_{1}}g_{B}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}
,
for the special location
\bm{x}={(-L_{1x}/2,0,z)}^{T}
.
Hence
{g}^{-1}_{A_{1}}g_{B}
has a fixed point and this case is invalid.
A similar argument for
m^{(2)}_{3}=1
leads to the requirement
L_{1z}=L_{2z}=0
.

Restoring an arbitrary origin (which reintroduces
L_{Bx}
and
L_{By}
), we are left with the generic result quoted in the main text (
3.2
),

\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1x}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{2x}\\
L_{2y}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Bx}\\
L_{By}\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.14)

or equivalently (
3.6
),

\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{A_{1}}=L_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{A_{2}}=L_{2}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\alpha\\
\sin\alpha\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{B}=L_{B}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\beta\cos\gamma\\
\cos\beta\sin\gamma\\
\sin\beta\end{pmatrix}.

(A.15)

A.3
E_{3}
: Quarter-turn space

The quarter-turn space is similar to
E_{2}
.
Since there is a quarter turn, instead of a half turn, the computations are a bit more involved, but follow similar logic.
Here we will explicitly impose the ordering conditions discussed in the main text.

To begin we first use the freedom to rotate the coordinate system such that the axis of rotation of
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}
is parallel to
\bm{\hat{e}}_{z}
, and then to choose
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
to be in the
xz
-plane.
For
E_{3}

\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(\pi/2)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&\hphantom{-}0&0\\
0&\hphantom{-}0&1\end{pmatrix},

(A.16)

which has the properties

(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}=\diag(-1,-1,1),\quad(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{3}={\bm{\mathsf{M}}}^{T}_{B},\quad(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{4}=\mathds{1}.

(A.17)

Once again
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{A}=\mathds{1}
, so
g_{A_{1}}
and
g_{A_{2}}
are pure translations by
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
and
\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
, respectively.
We therefore assign them the alternative labels
\bm{T}_{1}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
and
\bm{T}_{2}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
.
Finally, as in
E_{2}
we can shift to the origin where
L_{Bx}=L_{By}=0
and again have as our starting case

\bm{T}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1x}\\
0\\
L_{1z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{2x}\\
L_{2y}\\
L_{2z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.18)

Similar to
E_{2}
, an associated
E_{1}
can be constructed from three pure linearly independent translations.
We again have the obvious translation vectors
\bm{T}_{1}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
and
\bm{T}_{2}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
.
A third translation vector can be defined based on the fact that
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{3}}_{B})^{4}=\mathds{1}
.
Noting that

g^{E_{3}}_{3}\equiv(g^{E_{3}}_{B})^{4}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{3}}_{04}\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{B},

(A.19)

where
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{3}}_{04}=\diag(0,0,4)
from (
4.35
),
the third translation vector of the associated
E_{1}
for
E_{3}
is, as quoted in the main text (
3.14
),

\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{3}={(0,0,4L_{Bz})}^{T}.

(A.20)

For our subsequent development here we choose
\bm{T}_{3}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{3}
.

To proceed we can always choose
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
such that

|\bm{T}_{A_{1}\perp}|\leq|\bm{T}_{A_{2}\perp}|
, where
\perp
means the projection into the
xy
-plane.
Also, by adding or subtracting integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{1}
from
\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
, we can ensure that
|L_{2x}|<|L_{1x}|
; thus we generically have

(L_{2x})^{2}<(L_{1x})^{2}\leq(L_{2x})^{2}+(L_{2y})^{2}.

(A.21)

Similarly, shifting by integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{3}
allows us to insist that
|L_{1z}|\leq 2|L_{Bz}|
and
|L_{2z}|\leq 2|L_{Bz}|
.

Given the basis set for the associated
E_{1}
of
\bm{T}_{1}
,
\bm{T}_{2}
, and
\bm{T}_{3}
we can proceed to find the most general action of the generators.
Whereas for
E_{2}
we had one set of conditions (
A.9
) on the products of generators and their inverses being integer linear combination of
\bm{T}_{i}^{E_{2}}
, for
E_{3}
we have two independent sets, which we can take for
i
and
j\in\{1,2\}
to be

\displaystyle{g}_{B}{g}^{-1}_{A_{i}}{g}^{-1}_{B}g_{A_{j}}:\bm{x}

\displaystyle\to\bm{x}+\bm{T}_{A_{j}}-{\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}=\bm{x}+\bm{T}_{A_{j}}+\sum_{k}m^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},

(A.22)

\displaystyle({g}^{-1}_{B})^{2}{g}^{-1}_{A_{i}}(g_{B})^{2}g_{A_{j}}:\bm{x}

\displaystyle\to\bm{x}+\bm{T}_{A_{j}}-(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}=\bm{x}+\bm{T}_{A_{j}}+\sum_{k}n^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k}.

(A.23)

Just like in
E_{2}
, these reduce to

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}m^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }m^{(i)}_{k}\in\mathbb{Z},

(A.24)

\displaystyle(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}n^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }n^{(i)}_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(A.25)

Since
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A_{1}}=\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
, and since we could have used
{g}^{-1}_{B}{g}^{-1}_{A_{i}}{g_{B}}g_{A_{j}}
instead of
{g}_{B}{g}^{-1}_{A_{i}}{g_{B}}^{-1}g_{A_{j}}
for the left-hand side of (
A.22
), which would have replaced
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}
with
{\bm{\mathsf{M}}}^{T}_{B}=\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}^{3}
on the right-hand side of (
A.22
),
we need to only consider (
A.24
).
Thus, despite all the apparent freedom, this once again leads to only two sets of conditions:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
L_{1x}\\
L_{1z}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}m^{(1)}_{1}L_{1x}+m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2x}\\
m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2y}\\
m^{(1)}_{1}L_{1z}+m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2z}+m^{(1)}_{3}4L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix},

(A.26)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-L_{2y}\\
\hphantom{-}L_{2x}\\
\hphantom{-}L_{2z}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}m^{(2)}_{1}L_{1x}+m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2x}\\
m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2y}\\
m^{(2)}_{1}L_{1z}+m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2z}+m^{(2)}_{3}4L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(A.27)

As in
E_{2}
we must have
L_{2y}\neq 0
.
From the
y
-component of (
A.26
) we see immediately that

L_{1x}=m_{2}^{(1)}L_{2y}\neq 0.

(A.28)

To show this, suppose that
L_{1x}=0
.
From (
A.28
) this means that
m_{2}^{(1)}=0
.
Applying these relations to the
x
- and
y
-components of (
A.27
) leads to
L_{2y}=-(m^{(2)}_{2})^{2}L_{2y}
.
Since
L_{2y}\neq 0
this would then require
m^{(2)}_{2}=0
, but from the
x
-component of (
A.27
) this would force
L_{2y}=0
, which is inconsistent.
Thus,
L_{1x}\neq 0
and we must have
m_{2}^{(1)}\neq 0
.

Proceeding, we note that
|\bm{T}_{A_{1}\perp}|=|L_{1x}|=|m^{(1)}_{2}||L_{2y}|
and
|\bm{T}_{A_{2}\perp}|=\sqrt{L_{2x}^{2}+L_{2y}^{2}}\leq|L_{2y}|
.
Thus
|\bm{T}_{A_{1}\perp}|\leq|\bm{T}_{A_{2}\perp}|
requires
|m_{2}^{(1)}|\leq 1
, which means that
m_{2}^{(1)}=\pm 1
.
Next, from the
x
-component of (
A.26
), we require
L_{2x}=-m_{1}^{(1)}L_{2y}
.
However, we can always add or subtract integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{1}
from
\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
, so
|L_{2x}|
cannot be larger than
\frac{1}{2}L_{1x}
, but again, since
|\bm{T}_{A_{1}\perp}|\leq|\bm{T}_{A_{2}\perp}|
, we must have

0<L_{1x}^{2}\leq L_{2x}^{2}+L_{2y}^{2}=\frac{1+(m_{1}^{(1)})^{2}}{(m_{1}^{(1)})^{2}}L_{2x}^{2}\leq\frac{1+(m_{1}^{(1)})^{2}}{(m_{1}^{(1)})^{2}}\frac{L_{1x}^{2}}{4}\,.

(A.29)

This is satisfied if and only if
m_{1}^{(1)}=0
.
Since
m_{2}^{(1)}\neq 0
, the
x
-component of (
A.26
) informs us that

L_{2x}=0.

(A.30)

The
y
-component of (
A.27
) then requires that
m_{2}^{(2)}=0
.

Next, the
x
-component of (
A.27
) together with (
A.28
) and
m_{2}^{(2)}=0

gives us that
m_{1}^{(2)}m_{2}^{(1)}=-1
, thus (since
m_{2}^{(1)}=\pm 1
) we have
m_{1}^{(2)}=-m_{2}^{(1)}=\mp 1
.
We thus conclude that

L_{2y}=\pm L_{1x}.

(A.31)

The
z
-components of the two conditions now give us

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-L_{1z}\pm L_{2z}+m_{3}^{(1)}2L_{Bz},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mp L_{1z}-L_{2z}+m_{3}^{(2)}4L_{Bz}.

(A.32)

Thus
L_{1z}
and
L_{2z}
are both integer multiples of
L_{Bz}
.
But, as in
E_{2}
, unless these integers are both
0
, at least one of
{g}^{-1}_{A_{1}}(g_{B})^{n}
for
n\in\{1,2,3\}
will have a fixed point.
From this, we can show that we must have

L_{1z}=L_{2z}=0.

(A.33)

Putting all of this together, shifting to an arbitrary origin, and renaming
L_{1x}
to
L_{A}
we arrive at the general form from the text (
3.3
)

\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{A}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
L_{A}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Bx}\\
L_{By}\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.34)

Note that we might have thought we should put
\pm L_{A}
in
\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{A_{2}}
, however this can be removed by rotating about the
z
-axis by
\pi/2
or by replacing
\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{A_{2}}
with
-\bm{T}^{E_{3}}_{A_{2}}
.

A.4
E_{4}
: Third-turn space

The third-turn space is similar to the previous two cases, particularly
E_{3}
. from which the development follows.
We again begin by using the rotational freedom to set the axis of
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}
parallel to
\bm{\hat{e}}_{z}
and then to choose
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
to be in the
xz
-plane.
For
E_{4}

\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(2\pi/3)=\begin{pmatrix}-1/2&-\sqrt{3}/2&0\\
\sqrt{3}/2&-1/2&0\\
0&\hphantom{-}0&1\end{pmatrix},

(A.35)

which has the properties

(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}={\bm{\mathsf{M}}}^{T}_{B}\mbox{ and }(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{3}=\mathds{1}.

(A.36)

As in
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
and shifting the origin so that
L_{Bx}=L_{By}=0
, we again start with

\bm{T}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1x}\\
0\\
L_{1z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{2x}\\
L_{2y}\\
L_{2z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.37)

Similar to
E_{2}
, an associated
E_{1}
can be constructed from three pure translations.
We again have the obvious translation vectors
\bm{T}_{1}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
and
\bm{T}_{2}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
.
A third translation vector can be defined based on the fact that
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{4}}_{B})^{3}=\mathds{1}
.
Noting that

g^{E_{4}}_{3}\equiv(g^{E_{4}}_{B})^{3}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{4}}_{03}\bm{T}^{E_{4}}_{B},

(A.38)

where
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{4}}_{03}=\diag(0,0,3)
from (
4.35
),
the third translation vector of the associated
E_{1}
for
E_{4}
is as quoted in the main text (
3.20
),

\bm{T}^{E_{4}}_{3}={(0,0,3L_{Bz})}^{T}.

(A.39)

For our subsequent development here, we choose
\bm{T}_{3}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{4}}_{3}
.

To proceed, we can always choose
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
such that
|\bm{T}_{A_{1}\perp}|\leq|\bm{T}_{A_{2}\perp}|
, where
\perp
means the projection into the
xy
-plane.
Also, by adding or subtracting integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{1}
from
\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
, we can ensure that
|L_{2x}|<|L_{1x}|
; thus we generically have

(L_{2x})^{2}<(L_{1x})^{2}\leq(L_{2x})^{2}+(L_{2y})^{2}.

(A.40)

Since
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}^{2}={\bm{\mathsf{M}}}^{T}_{B}
, we only need to consider the condition with one factor of
g_{B}
,

{g}^{-1}_{B}{g}^{-1}_{A_{i}}g_{B}g_{A_{j}}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\bm{T}_{A_{j}}-{\bm{\mathsf{M}}}^{T}_{B}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}=\bm{x}+\sum_{k}m^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},

(A.41)

and not the condition analogous to (
A.23
).
Again this reduces to

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}=\sum_{k}m^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }m_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z},

(A.42)

and again leads to the two sets of conditions

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}L_{1x}\\
-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}L_{1x}\\
L_{1z}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}m^{(1)}_{1}L_{1x}+m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2x}\\
m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2y}\\
m^{(1)}_{1}L_{1z}+m^{(1)}_{2}L_{2z}+m^{(1)}_{3}3L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix},

(A.43)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}L_{2x}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}L_{2y}\\
\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}L_{2x}-\frac{1}{2}L_{2y}\\
L_{2z}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}m^{(2)}_{1}L_{1x}+m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2x}\\
m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2y}\\
m^{(2)}_{1}L_{1z}+m^{(2)}_{2}L_{2z}+m^{(2)}_{3}3L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(A.44)

Similar to
E_{3}
, the
y
-component of (
A.43
) requires that
m^{(1)}_{2}\neq 0
.
To see this, we note that
m^{(1)}_{2}=0
means that
L_{1x}=0
.
Applying these two relations to the
x
- and
y
-components of (
A.44
) leads to contradictions.
Explicitly, if
m^{(2)}_{2}=0
then
L_{2y}=0
, whereas if
m^{(2)}_{2}\neq 0
then
(L_{2x}/L_{2y})^{2}=-1
; neither of these are valid solutions.
Thus we require
m^{(1)}_{2}\neq 0
.

Returning to the
x
- and
y
-components of (
A.43
) we have

L_{2x}=-\frac{2m_{1}^{(1)}+1}{2m_{2}^{(1)}}L_{1x}\mbox{ and }L_{2y}=-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2m_{2}^{(1)}}L_{1x}\,,

(A.45)

for
m_{1}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m_{2}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\neq 0}
.
For (
A.40
) to be satisfied with these relations, we must have

(m_{1}^{(1)})^{2}+m_{1}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{4}\leq(m_{2}^{(1)})^{2}\leq(m_{1}^{(1)})^{2}+m_{1}^{(1)}+1.

(A.46)

The only (integer) solutions to these inequalities are

m_{1}^{(1)}\in\{-1,0\}\mbox{ and }m_{2}^{(1)}=\pm 1.

(A.47)

These translate to

L_{2x}=\pm\frac{1}{2}L_{1x}\mbox{ and }L_{2y}=\pm\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}L_{1x},

(A.48)

with independent sign choices.
Plugging the expressions for
L_{2x}
and
L_{2y}
into the
x
- and
y
-components of (
A.44
) leads to

m_{1}^{(2)}\in\{-1,0\}\mbox{ and }m_{2}^{(2)}=\pm 1.

(A.49)

The many choices for the sets of integers
m^{(i)}_{j}
with
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, when plugged into the
z
-components of (
A.43
) and (
A.44
), lead to the requirements that
L_{1z}
and
L_{2z}
must be integer multiples of
L_{Bz}
.
We can always make these integer multiples
0
,
1
, or
2
by adding or subtracting integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{3}
.
But, as in the previous cases, choosing either
1
or
2
leads to fix points.
Thus
L_{1z}=L_{2z}=0
.

The independent choices of signs for
L_{2x}
and
L_{2y}
are equivalent as it is immaterial whether we take
\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
or
-\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
, since it is a pure translation.
Using this, we can always choose
L_{2y}>0
.
Finally, we can always switch between
L_{2x}=-\frac{1}{2}L_{1x}
and
L_{2x}=+\frac{1}{2}L_{1x}
by adding (subtracting)
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
to (from)
\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
.
We choose
L_{2x}=-\frac{1}{2}L_{1x}
.

Putting all of this together, shifting to an arbitrary origin, and renaming
L_{1x}
to
L_{A}
we arrive at the general form from the text (
3.4
),

\bm{T}^{E_{4}}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{A}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{4}}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}-L_{A}/2\\
\sqrt{3}L_{A}/2\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{4}}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Bx}\\
L_{By}\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.50)

A.5
E_{5}
: Sixth-turn space

The sixth turn space is very much like
E_{4}
, though with a richer matrix structure.
We begin by using the rotational freedom to set the axis of
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}
parallel to
\bm{\hat{e}}_{z}
and then to choose
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
to be in the
xz
-plane.
For
E_{5}

\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(\pi/3)=\begin{pmatrix}1/2&-\sqrt{3}/2&0\\
\sqrt{3}/2&1/2&0\\
0&\hphantom{-}0&1\end{pmatrix},

(A.51)

which has the properties

\displaystyle(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(2\pi/3),\quad(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{3}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(\pi)=\diag(-1,-1,1),

\displaystyle(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{4}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(4\pi/3)=({\bm{\mathsf{M}}}^{T}_{B})^{2},\quad(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{5}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(5\pi/3)={\bm{\mathsf{M}}}^{T}_{B},\quad(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{6}=\mathds{1}.

(A.52)

As in the previous cases, we shift the origin so that
L_{Bx}=L_{By}=0
, and we again start with

\bm{T}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1x}\\
0\\
L_{1z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{2x}\\
L_{2y}\\
L_{2z}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.53)

An associated
E_{1}
can again be constructed from three pure translations,
starting with the obvious translation vectors
\bm{T}_{1}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
and
\bm{T}_{2}\equiv\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
.
A third translation vector can be defined based on the fact that
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{5}}_{B})^{6}=\mathds{1}
.
Noting that

g^{E_{5}}_{3}\equiv(g^{E_{5}}_{B})^{6}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{5}}_{06}\bm{T}^{E_{5}}_{B},

(A.54)

where
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{5}}_{06}=\diag(0,0,6)
from (
4.35
),
the third translation vector of the associated
E_{1}
for
E_{5}
is as quoted in the main text (
3.26
),

\bm{T}^{E_{5}}_{3}={(0,0,6L_{Bz})}^{T}.

(A.55)

For our subsequent development here we choose
\bm{T}_{3}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{5}}_{3}
.

To proceed, we can again choose
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
such that
|\bm{T}_{A_{1}\perp}|\leq|\bm{T}_{A_{2}\perp}|
, where
\perp
means the projection into the
xy
-plane.
Also, by adding or subtracting integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{A_{1}}
from
\bm{T}_{A_{2}}
, we can ensure that
|L_{2x}|<|L_{1x}|
; thus we generically have

(L_{2x})^{2}<(L_{1x})^{2}\leq(L_{2x})^{2}+(L_{2y})^{2}.

(A.56)

Whereas for
E_{4}
we had two sets of conditions, (
A.41
), on the products of generators and their inverses being integer linear combination of
\bm{T}_{i}^{E_{4}}
, which we were able to limit to one, for
E_{5}
we have 5 sets, which we can limit to 3

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}m^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }m_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z},

(A.57)

\displaystyle(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}n^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }n_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z},

(A.58)

\displaystyle(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{3}\bm{T}_{A_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}p^{(i)}_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }p_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(A.59)

This follows since the condition for
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{4}
is equivalent to that for
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}
and the condition for
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{5}
is equivalent to that for
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}
.

This leads to a daunting 6 sets of conditions.
However, if we focus on the
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}
equations (
A.58
) we see they are identical to the conditions from
E_{4}
(
A.41
), thus the solution is the same and we have an immediate candidate for the general solution.
By direct computation, we can verify that this solution also satisfies (
A.57
) and (
A.59
), so there are no additional constraints.

Finally, shifting to an arbitrary origin, as in
E_{4}
we have the general form from the text (
3.5
),

\bm{T}^{E_{5}}_{A_{1}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{A}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{5}}_{A_{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}-L_{A}/2\\
\sqrt{3}L_{A}/2\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{5}}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Bx}\\
L_{By}\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.60)

A.6
E_{6}
: Hantzsche-Wendt space

In contrast to the other orientable, compact spaces, the Hantzsche-Wendt space has rotations around multiple axes.
These are conventionally chosen as half turns about three axes which must be orthogonal (see, e.g., Ref.
[
18
]
).
Here, we use the freedom to rotate the coordinate system such that these three axes are aligned with the coordinate axes choosing
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{A}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{x}}}(\pi)
,
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{y}}}(\pi)
, and
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{C}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(\pi)
. For
E_{6}
we then have

\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}0\\
0&-1&\hphantom{-}0\\
0&\hphantom{-}0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}0\\
\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}1&\hphantom{-}0\\
\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{C}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}0\\
\hphantom{-}0&-1&\hphantom{-}0\\
\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}0&\hphantom{-}1\end{pmatrix},

(A.61)

so that

(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{A})^{2}=(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B})^{2}=(\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{C})^{2}=\mathds{1}.

(A.62)

This has used up all the rotational freedom.
There is still the freedom to choose a special origin so as to simplify the translation vectors (as we have done in the previous cases), however, we will begin without using this freedom and thus start with the most general vectors

\bm{T}_{a}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{ax}\\
L_{ay}\\
L_{az}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{for }a\in\{A,B,C\}.

(A.63)

An associated
E_{1}
can again be constructed from three pure translations.
In contrast to
E_{2}
–
E_{5}
, the action of each generator in
E_{6}
includes a rotation.
However, since the rotations are all half turns, we can choose the three translations as was done for
g^{E_{2}}_{3}
.
From the properties of the matrices
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
, we construct the pure translations from
(g_{a})^{2}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+(\mathds{1}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{a})\bm{T}_{a}
.
This leads to the pure translation vectors quoted in the main text (
3.6
),

\bm{T}_{1}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{6}}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}2L_{Ax}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{2}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{6}}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
2L_{By}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{3}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{6}}_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
2L_{Cz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.64)

To proceed, we note that
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{a}\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{b}=\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{c}
for any
\{a,b,c\}
permutation of
\{A,B,C\}
, which means that we must have
g_{a}g_{b}=g_{c}+\bm{t}
for
\bm{t}
a pure translation constructed from integer linear combinations of the
\bm{T}_{i}
.
This leads to the set of conditions

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{A}\bm{T}_{B}+\bm{T}_{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{T}_{C}+\sum_{i}m_{i}^{C}\bm{T}_{i},

(A.65)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{C}+\bm{T}_{B}

\displaystyle=\bm{T}_{A}+\sum_{i}m_{i}^{A}\bm{T}_{i},

(A.66)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{C}\bm{T}_{A}+\bm{T}_{C}

\displaystyle=\bm{T}_{B}+\sum_{i}m_{i}^{B}\bm{T}_{i},

(A.67)

for some
m^{a}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Written out explicitly, these conditions are

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\hphantom{-}L_{Bx}+L_{Ax}\\
-L_{By}+L_{Ay}\\
-L_{Bz}+L_{Az}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}2m^{C}_{1}L_{Ax}+L_{Cx}\\
2m^{C}_{2}L_{By}+L_{Cy}\\
2m^{C}_{3}L_{Cz}+L_{Cz}\end{pmatrix},

(A.68)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-L_{Cx}+L_{Bx}\\
\hphantom{-}L_{Cy}+L_{By}\\
-L_{Cz}+L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}2m^{A}_{1}L_{Ax}+L_{Ax}\\
2m^{A}_{2}L_{By}+L_{Ay}\\
2m^{A}_{3}L_{Cz}+L_{Az}\end{pmatrix},

(A.69)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-L_{Ax}+L_{Cx}\\
-L_{Ay}+L_{Cy}\\
\hphantom{-}L_{Az}+L_{Cz}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}2m^{B}_{1}L_{Ax}+L_{Bx}\\
2m^{B}_{2}L_{By}+L_{By}\\
2m^{B}_{3}L_{Cz}+L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.70)

From these, we first focus on the
x
-component of (
A.69
), the
y
-component of (
A.70
), and the
z
-component of (
A.68
). This gives

\displaystyle L_{Cx}-L_{Bx}

\displaystyle=-(2m^{A}_{1}+1)L_{Ax},

\displaystyle L_{Ay}-L_{Cy}

\displaystyle=-(2m^{B}_{2}+1)L_{By},

(A.71)

\displaystyle L_{Bz}-L_{Az}

\displaystyle=-(2m^{C}_{3}+1)L_{Cz},

reducing the original set to 6 parameters.
Shifting by integer multiples of the translation vectors
\bm{T}_{i}
allows us to remove all the even multiples on the right-hand side of these expressions.
In particular, the choice
m^{A}_{1}=m^{B}_{2}=m^{C}_{3}=-1
reproduces the general form from the text (
3.6
),

\bm{T}^{E_{6}}_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Ax}\\
L_{By}+L_{Cy}\\
L_{Az}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{6}}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Bx}\\
L_{By}\\
L_{Cz}+L_{Az}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{6}}_{C}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Ax}+L_{Bx}\\
L_{Cy}\\
L_{Cz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.72)

The behavior of the generators under a shift of origin suggests an alternative form for the translation vectors.
A shift in the origin preserves the difference between the lengths on the left-hand side of (
A.6
) but not their sums.
(In fact, this is another way to understand why the three lengths on the right-hand sides appear in the translation vectors of the associated
E_{1}
(
3.6
).)
The sums of the lengths can be used to parametrize the generators in another form.
Notice that the sum of the
x
-components of (
A.68
) and (
A.70
) gives

L_{Bx}+L_{Cx}=2(m^{C}_{1}+m^{B}_{1})L_{Ax}+L_{Bx}+L_{Cx},

(A.73)

in other words that

2(m^{C}_{1}+m^{B}_{1})L_{Ax}=0.

(A.74)

But this is precisely the freedom used above to shift by integer multiples of
\bm{T}_{1}
.
In other words, the sum of lengths
L_{Bx}+L_{Cx}
is unconstrained by the set of conditions.
Similar arguments hold for the sums
L_{Cy}+L_{Ay}
and
L_{Az}+L_{Bz}
.
This motivates the definition of new continuous, dimensionless parameters given by the average of these length pairs as

\displaystyle r_{x}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{L_{Bx}+L_{Cx}}{2L_{Ax}}=\frac{2L_{Bx}+L_{Ax}}{2L_{Ax}},

\displaystyle r_{y}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{L_{Cy}+L_{Ay}}{2L_{By}}=\frac{2L_{Cy}+L_{By}}{2L_{By}},

(A.75)

\displaystyle r_{z}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{L_{Az}+L_{Bz}}{2L_{Cz}}=\frac{2L_{Az}+L_{Cz}}{2L_{Cz}},

where the second equality in each line follows from the same choices that went into the general form (
3.6
).
Solving for one of the lengths in each equation, we can write these as

L_{Bx}=\left(r_{x}-\frac{1}{2}\right)L_{Ax},\quad L_{Cy}=\left(r_{y}-\frac{1}{2}\right)L_{By},\quad L_{Az}=\left(r_{z}-\frac{1}{2}\right)L_{Cz}.

(A.76)

Finally, these give the alternative form quoted in the main text (
3.30
),

\bm{T}_{A}^{E_{6}}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Ax}\\
(r_{y}+\frac{1}{2})L_{By}\\
(r_{z}-\frac{1}{2})L_{Cz}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{B}^{E_{6}}=\begin{pmatrix}(r_{x}-\frac{1}{2})L_{Ax}\\
L_{By}\\
(r_{z}+\frac{1}{2})L_{Cz}\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{C}^{E_{6}}=\begin{pmatrix}(r_{x}+\frac{1}{2})L_{Ax}\\
(r_{y}-\frac{1}{2})L_{By}\\
L_{Cz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.77)

A.7
E_{11}
: Chimney space

The general form of the generators for the chimney space can be determined in a manner similar to
E_{1}
, though it requires two generators, not three.
Since
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{11}}=\mathds{1}
,
E_{11}
is homogeneous and we can use the rotational freedom to define one of the translation vectors as the
x
-axis of the coordinate system and the remaining freedom to define the
xy
-plane.
In other words, two vectors define a plane so the two translation vectors can be used to define the
xy
-plane.
Thus the two translations
\bm{T}^{E_{1}}_{i}
can be parametrized most generally as quoted in the text (
3.7
),

\bm{T}^{E_{11}}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1x}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{11}}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{2x}\\
L_{2y}\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(A.78)

or equivalently

\bm{T}^{E_{11}}_{1}=L_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{11}}_{2}=L_{2}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\alpha\\
\sin\alpha\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

(A.79)

A.8
E_{12}
: Chimney space with half turn

The chimney space with half turn is a root of
E_{11}
and is like the half-turn space
E_{2}
with one non-compact dimension.
It has one generator,
g_{A}
, that is a translation and one generator,
g_{B}
, that is a corkscrew motion.
The rotational freedom to align the coordinate system allows the axis of rotation to be chosen as the
y
-axis.
Freedom to rotate around the
y
-axis allows the choice
T_{Az}=0
.
Finally, we can choose a special origin that allows us to set
L_{Bx}=L_{Bz}=0
; we will restore an arbitrary origin at the end of the calculation.
With all of this, we can choose our general starting case to be

\bm{T}_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Ax}\\
L_{Ay}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
L_{By}\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

(A.80)

From these, a set of pure translations, an associated
E_{11}
, can be constructed.
The action of
g_{A}
is already a pure translation, so one of the needed translation vectors can be chosen as
\bm{T}_{1}\equiv\bm{T}_{A}
.
A second translation vector can be defined based on the fact that
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{12}}_{B})^{2}=\mathds{1}
.
Note that

g^{E_{12}}_{2}\equiv(g^{E_{12}}_{B})^{2}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+(\mathds{1}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{12}}_{B})\bm{T}^{E_{12}}_{B}\equiv\bm{x}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{12}}_{02}\bm{T}^{E_{12}}_{B},

(A.81)

for
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{12}}_{02}=\diag(0,2,0)
from (
4.35
).
Thus the second translation vector of the associated
E_{11}
for
E_{12}
is as quoted in the main text (
3.42
),

\bm{T}^{E_{12}}_{2}\equiv{(0,2L_{By},0)}^{T}.

(A.82)

For our subsequent development here we choose
\bm{T}_{2}\equiv\bm{T}^{E_{12}}_{2}
.

The remaining condition to impose is that

{g}^{-1}_{B}{g}^{-1}_{A}g_{B}g_{A}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\bm{T}_{A}-\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A}=\bm{x}+\sum_{k}m_{k}\bm{T}_{k},

(A.83)

for
m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
\bm{T}_{A}
is already the pure translation
\bm{T}_{1}
, this is equivalent to requiring

\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}\bm{T}_{A}=\sum_{k}m_{k}\bm{T}_{k},\quad\mbox{for some }m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z},

(A.84)

leading to the set of conditions

\begin{pmatrix}-L_{Ax}\\
\hphantom{-}L_{Ay}\\
\hphantom{-}0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}L_{Ax}\\
m_{1}L_{Ay}+2m_{2}L_{By}\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

(A.85)

From the
x
-component of these conditions, we see that
m_{1}=-1
.
Applying this to the
y
-component we immediately see that
L_{Ay}=0
(and
m_{2}=0
) is the general solution.
Shifting back to an arbitrary origin we have the generic result quoted in the main text (
3.8
),

\bm{T}^{E_{12}}_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Ax}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{T}^{E_{12}}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{Bx}\\
L_{By}\\
L_{Bz}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.86)

As stated, we notice that
E_{12}
is just a special case of
E_{2}
where
|\bm{T}^{E_{2}}_{A_{2}}|\to\infty
, with the conventional change that
\bm{\mathsf{M}}_{B}
is now a rotation about the
y
-axis instead of the
z
-axis.
As shown above, we were able to use the same arguments as for
E_{2}
(with
y
and
z
swapped) to argue that
L_{Ay}=0
, or equivalently that
\alpha=0
.

A.9
E_{16}
: Slab space including rotations

The slab space is distinct from the other topologies as it allows a corkscrew motion.
The standard definition of the slab space does not contain this rotation since the rotated slab is topologically the same as the unrotated slab.
However, a rotation does lead to physically observable effects, and thus unrotated and rotated slab manifolds must be treated separately.
For this reason, we split the description of
E_{16}
into two cases labeled
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
and
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}
.

A.9.1
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
: Conventional unrotated slab space

The general form of the generators for the unrotated slab space follows directly from the fact that it contains only a single generator that is a pure translation.
Since
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}}_{A}=\mathds{1}
,
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
is homogeneous and we can use the rotational freedom to define the translation vector as the
z
-axis of the coordinate system.
Thus we immediately arrive at the general form quoted in the text (
3.44
),

\bm{T}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}}_{A}=L{(0,0,1)}^{T}.

(A.87)

A.9.2
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}
: General rotated slab space

An arbitrary rotation by an angle
\zeta
is allowed in the slab space.
We can use part of the rotational freedom to choose the
z
-axis of the coordinate system as the rotation axis.
We can then use the freedom of rotation about this axis to set the
y
-component of the translation vector to zero.
Thus we can start with

\bm{\mathsf{M}}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(\zeta),\quad\mbox{with}\quad\bm{T}={(L_{x},0,L_{z})}^{T}.

(A.88)

In principle
0<\zeta<2\pi
(where the endpoints are excluded so that
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}
does not include
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
) and this would be the general action of a group element.
However, in this work, we focus on the observable consequences of our universe having a non-trivial topology.
To this end, in
Section

4
the eigenmodes of each topology are determined.
In order to use a Fourier basis to expand all functions on the manifold, a required condition is (effectively) that
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{q}=\mathds{1}
for some finite
q\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
.
This in turn requires that
q\zeta=2\pi p
for
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{\neq 0}
.
In other words,
\zeta
must be a rational multiple of
2\pi
.
If
\zeta
is not a rational multiple of
2\pi
then only cylindrically symmetric functions can be expanded in a Fourier basis.
The translation vector given above is general, it is the rotation matrix that has further constraints.
With this, we arrive at the general form given in the text (
3.9.2
),

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{B}=\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{\bm{\hat{z}}}(2\pi p/q)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(2\pi p/q)&-\sin(2\pi p/q)&0\\
\sin(2\pi p/q)&\hphantom{-}\cos(2\pi p/q)&0\\
0&\hphantom{-}0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mbox{with}

\displaystyle\bm{T}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{B}={(L_{x},0,L_{z})}^{T}=L{(\cos\beta,0,\sin\beta)}^{T},

(A.89)

and
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{\neq 0}
,
q\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
, and
|p|
and
q
relatively prime.
From this, a pure translation, an associated
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
, can be constructed.
Based on the fact that
(\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{B})^{q}=\mathds{1}
we note that

(g^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{B})^{q}:\bm{x}\to\bm{x}+\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{0q}\bm{T}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{B},

(A.90)

for
\bm{\mathsf{M}}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{0q}=\diag(0,0,q)
from (
4.35
).
Thus the translation vector of the associated
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{h})}
for
E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}
is as quoted in the main text (
3.46
),

\bm{T}^{E_{16}^{(\mathrm{i})}}_{1}={(0,0,qL_{z})}^{T}.

(A.91)
